. Since the mass of the gas is left unchanged and
\rho=M/\mathrm{V}
it follows that

(5.11)

\displaystyle p\sim|\rho|^{5/3}

and so an adiabatic perfect gas is also a polytropic gas with

p=\mathsf{K}|\rho|^{5/3}
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

The polytropic gas model can be applied to the following types of stars:

•

Stars with efficient convective energy transport obey a polytropic law

p=\mathsf{K}|\rho|^{5/3}
so that
\gamma=5/3
and
\mathsf{K}
depends on boundary conditions such as
p(0),\rho(0)
.

•

Light white dwarf stars with
\gamma\sim 5/3
and very heavy white dwarf stars with
\gamma\sim 4/3
. Then
\mathsf{K}
depends on chemical composition and fundamental constants, and the equation of state can expressed in polytropic form.

•

Supermassive stars supported predominantly by radiation pressure with

p=\mathsf{K}|\rho|^{4/3}
, and
\mathsf{K}
depends on the ratio of matter and radiation pressure.

The following formulas for
\mathbf{E}_{G}
and
\mathbf{E}_{T}
for a polytropic gas star are well known and applies the FEHE
\bm{[1,10,12]}
.

Theorem 5.4
.

(
\underline{Betti~and~Ritter}
. The gravitational potential energy
\mathbf{E}_{G}
and the thermal energy
\mathbf{E}_{T}
for a polytropic gaseous star with polytropic equation of state

p(r)=\mathsf{K}|\rho(r)|^{\gamma}=\mathsf{K}|\rho(r)|^{\frac{n+1}{n}}
is

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{G}=-\int_{0}^{M}\frac{\mathscr{G}\mathcal{M}(r)d\mathcal{M}(r)}{r}=\frac{3}{5-n}\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{R}\equiv-\frac{3(\gamma-1)}{(5\gamma-6)}\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{R}

(5.13)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{T}=\int_{0}^{R}\mathcal{E}4\pi r^{2}dr=\frac{1}{(5\gamma-1)}\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{R}=-\frac{1}{(3\gamma-1)}\mathbf{E}_{G}

The total energy is then

(5.14)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{E}_{T}+\mathbf{E}_{G}=-\frac{(3\gamma-4)}{(5\gamma-6)}\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{R}

Proof.

The proof utilises the FEHE and the polytropic gas equation. From the polytropic state equation

(5.15)

\displaystyle\frac{dp(r)}{\rho(r)}=\mathsf{K}\left(\frac{n+1}{n}\right)|\rho(r)|^{\frac{1}{n-1}}d\rho(r)=(n+1)d\left(\frac{p(r)}{\rho(r)}\right)

Since there are several changes of variable, it is convenient to label the integrals throughout as
\int_{C}^{S}
, where C refers to any quantity at the centre and S refers to the surface. We take
p(R)=0,\rho(R)=0
and
\mathcal{M}(0)=0,\mathcal{M}(R)=M
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{M}\mathscr{G}\frac{\mathcal{M}(r)d\mathcal{M}(r)}{r}=-\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\int_{C}^{S}\frac{\mathscr{G}\mathcal{M}(r)d|\mathcal{M}(r)|^{2}}{r}}_{int~by~parts}

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\mathscr{G}{m}(r)}{2r}\right|_{C}^{S}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{C}^{S}\frac{\mathscr{G}|\mathcal{M}(r)|^{2}}{r^{2}}dr

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\mathscr{G}\mathcal{M}(r)}{2r}\right|_{C}^{S}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{C}^{S}\mathcal{M}(r)\underbrace{\left(\frac{\mathscr{G}\mathcal{M}(r)}{r^{2}}dr\right)}_{use~FEHE}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{2R}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{C}^{S}\mathcal{M}(r)\left(\frac{dp(r)}{\rho(r)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{2R}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}(n+1)\int_{C}^{S}\mathcal{M}(r)\left(\frac{dp(r)}{\rho(r)}\right)}_{int~by~parts}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{2R}+\left|\frac{1}{2}(n+1)\mathcal{M}(r)\frac{p(r)}{\rho(r)}\right|_{C}^{S}-\frac{1}{2}(n+1)\int_{C}^{S}\frac{p(r)}{\rho(r)}d\mathcal{M}(r)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{2R}-\frac{1}{2}(n+1)\int_{C}^{S}\frac{[p(r)}{\rho(r)}d\mathcal{M}(r)

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{2R}-\frac{1}{2}(n+1)\int_{C}^{S}\frac{p(r)}{\rho(r)}4\pi r^{2}\rho(r)dr

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{2R}-\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}(n+1)\int_{C}^{S}p(r)\frac{4\pi}{3}d|r^{3}|}_{int~by~parts}

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{2R}-\left|\frac{1}{2}(n+1)\frac{4\pi}{3}p(r)r^{3}\right|_{C}^{S}+\frac{1}{6}(n+1)\int_{C}^{S}\underbrace{4\pi r^{2}dp(r)}_{use~FEHE}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{2R}-\frac{1}{6}(n+1)\int_{C}^{S}4\pi r^{3}\frac{\mathscr{G}\mathcal{M}(r)}{r^{2}}\rho(r)dr

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{2R}-\frac{1}{6}(n+1)\int_{C}^{S}\frac{\mathscr{G}\mathcal{M}(r)d\mathcal{M}(r)}{r}

(5.16)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{2R}-\frac{1}{6}(n+1)\mathbf{E}_{G}

so

(5.17)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{G}=-\frac{GM^{2}}{2R}+\frac{1}{6}(n+1)\mathbf{E}_{G}

which then gives (5.12) as required. To prove (5.12), first note that for a polytropic gas
the energy density
\mathcal{E}
is related to the pressure by
\mathcal{E}(r)=(\gamma-1)^{-1}p(r)
. Then using the FEHE

(5.18)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{G}=4\pi\int_{0}^{R}r^{3}\frac{dp(r)}{dr}dr=-12\pi\int_{0}^{R}r^{2}p(r)dr=-12\pi\int_{0}^{R}\mathcal{E}(\gamma-1)r^{2}dr

Hence

(5.19)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{G}=-(\gamma-1)\mathbf{E}_{T}

and so (5.13) immediately follows using (5.12). The total energy
\mathbf{E}
also immediately follows.

Corollary 5.5
.

It can be see that
\mathbf{E}=0
if
\gamma=\tfrac{4}{3}
so such polytropic stars are teetering on the brink of catastrophic instability. Stability at least requires
\mathbf{E}<0
.

∎

5.1.
The Lane-Emden equations

Since the polytropic gas law is temperature independent, then the structure of such a star can be described by the FEHE in the Poisson form

(5.20)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{\rho(r)}\frac{dp(r)}{dr}\right)=-4\pi\mathscr{G}\rho

Using
p(r)=\mathsf{K}|\rho(r)|^{\gamma}
this becomes

(5.21)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{\rho(r)}\frac{d}{dr}|\rho(r)|^{\gamma}\right)+\frac{4\pi\mathscr{G}}{\mathsf{K}}r^{2}\rho=0

with the boundary conditions that
\rho(0)
has some prescribed value and
\rho^{\prime}(0)=0,\rho(R)=0
. We can state the following theorem for a polytropic gas equation of state
\bm{[13]}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\rho(r)
be a solution for
r_{o}\leq r<R
and let
[r_{o},R]
be a right maximal interval of existence for
\rho>0,R<\infty,|dp(r)/dr|_{r=r_{o}}<0
. Then
\exists C>0
such that

(5.22)

\displaystyle\rho(r)=C|R-r|^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}\left(1+\left\llbracket\left|\frac{R-r}{r}\right|,\overline{C}\left(\frac{R-r}{R}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}\right\rrbracket\right)

where
\overline{C}=QR^{\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}C^{2-\gamma}
and
Q=4\pi\mathscr{G}(\gamma-1)/\mathsf{K}\gamma
. Here,
\big\llbracket X\rrbracket
is a notation denoting a power series of the form
\llbracket X\rrbracket_{q}=\sum_{j\geq q}a_{j}X^{j}
and so

(5.23)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket X,Y\big\rrbracket_{q}=\sum_{j+k\geq q}q_{jk}X^{j}X^{k}

5.1.1.
Non-dimensional reduction

The Emden equation can be reduced to a dimensionless form, the Lane-Emden equation. This is a very standard derivation in the literature
\bm{1,2]}
. Defining a variable
\theta
by
\theta=|\rho(r)/\rho(0)|^{\gamma-1}
then

(5.24)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}\frac{d\theta(r)}{dr}\right)+\frac{4\pi\mathscr{G}(\gamma-1)}{\mathsf{K}\gamma}|\rho(0)|^{2-\gamma}|\theta(r)|^{1/(\gamma-1)}

Defining a second variable
\xi(r)
by

(5.25)

\displaystyle\xi(r)=\left|\frac{4\pi\mathscr{G}(\gamma-1)}{\mathsf{K}\gamma}\right|^{1/2}|\rho(0)|^{(2-\gamma)/2}r

the differential equation becomes

(5.26)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\frac{d}{d\xi}\left(\xi^{2}\frac{d}{d\xi}\theta(\xi)\right)+|\theta(\xi)|^{1/(\gamma-1)}=0

with boundary conditions
\theta(0)=1,\theta^{\prime}(0)=0
and
\theta(\xi_{1})=0
on the surface. Since
n=\frac{1}{\gamma}-1
the LE equation is identically.

(5.27)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\frac{d}{d\xi}\left(\xi^{2}\frac{d}{d\xi}\theta(\xi)\right)=-|\theta(\xi)|^{n}

This is then the Lane-Emden equation, which can be solved analytically or numerically depending on the polytropic index
n
. The following lemma establishes that if
\theta(\xi)=0
then one must have
[d\theta(\xi)/d\theta]_{\xi=0}=\theta^{\prime}(0)=0
; that is, the derivative must vanish at
\xi=0
for bounded solutions that are nonsingular at
\xi=0
. The function
\theta(\xi)
is analytic at
\xi=0
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Given the Lane-Emden equation

(5.28)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\frac{d}{d\xi}\left(\xi^{2}\frac{d\theta(\xi)}{d\theta}\right)\equiv\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}(\xi^{2}\theta^{\prime}(\xi))^{\prime}=f(\theta(\xi))

where
f(\theta(\xi))=-|\theta(\xi)|^{n}
. Let
f\in C(\mathbf{R})
and
\theta\in C^{2}(0,Q)
for some
Q>0
and
\theta\in[0,Q]
and
\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}(\xi^{2}\theta^{\prime}(\xi))^{\prime}=f(\theta)
. Then
\lim_{\xi\uparrow\infty}\theta^{\prime}(\xi)=0
if
\theta(0)=0
.

Proof.

Let
\xi\in[0,Q]
and express the LE equation as

(5.29)

\displaystyle(\xi^{2}\theta^{\prime}(\xi))^{\prime}=\xi^{2}f(\theta(\xi))

then integrating

(5.30)

\displaystyle\xi^{2}\theta^{\prime}(\xi)=\int_{\xi_{o}}^{\xi}\beta^{2}f(\theta(\beta))d\beta+\xi_{o}^{2}\theta(\xi_{o})

and

(5.31)

\displaystyle 0=\int_{\xi_{o}}^{0}\beta^{2}f(\theta(\beta))d\beta+\xi_{o}^{2}\theta(\xi_{o})

so that

(5.32)

\displaystyle\int_{\xi_{o}}^{0}\beta^{2}f(\theta(\beta))d\beta=-\xi_{o}^{2}\theta(\xi_{o})

Then (5.29) becomes

\displaystyle\xi^{2}\theta^{\prime}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\xi_{o}}^{\xi}\beta^{2}f(\theta(\beta))d\beta+\xi_{o}^{2}\theta(\xi_{o})

\displaystyle=\int_{\xi_{o}}^{0}\beta^{2}f(\theta(\beta)d\beta+\int_{0}^{\xi}\beta^{2}f(\theta(\beta))d\beta+\xi_{o}^{2}\theta(\xi_{o})

\displaystyle=-\int_{\xi}^{0}\beta^{2}f(\theta(\eta)d\eta+\int_{0}^{\xi}\beta^{2}f(\theta(\beta))d\beta+\xi_{o}^{2}\theta(\xi_{o})

(5.33)

\displaystyle=-\xi_{o}^{2}\theta(\xi_{o})+\xi_{o}^{2}\theta(\xi)+\int_{0}^{\xi}\beta^{2}f(\theta(\beta))d\beta=\int_{0}^{\xi}\beta^{2}f(\theta(\beta))d\beta

Now let
\mathcal{L}
be the limit as
\xi\rightarrow\infty

(5.34)

\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\uparrow 0}\xi^{2}\theta^{\prime}(\xi)=\mathcal{L}

and consider two cases:

(1)

Assume that the limit is strictly greater than zero so that
\mathcal{L}>0
. Then

(5.35)

\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\uparrow o}\theta^{\prime}(\xi)=\frac{\mathcal{L}}{\xi^{2}}

If some
\alpha\geq 0
then

(5.36)

\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\uparrow 0}\theta^{\prime}(\xi)\geq\frac{\mathcal{L}}{\alpha\xi^{2}}

and let
\xi\in[0,\epsilon]
. Integrating

(5.37)

\displaystyle(\theta(\epsilon)-\theta(0))=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}(\theta(\epsilon)-\theta(\xi))\geq\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\int_{\xi}^{\epsilon}\frac{\mathcal{L}}{\alpha\beta^{2}}d\beta=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\frac{\mathcal{L}}{\alpha\xi}-\frac{\mathcal{L}}{\alpha\epsilon}=-\infty

This then contradicts the requirement that
\theta(0)=0
. The derivative at
\xi=0
would then be singular and so does not exist.

(2)

The case
\mathcal{L}=0
. Now
\lim_{\xi\uparrow 0}\theta^{\prime}(\xi)=\mathcal{L}=0
. Using (5.30) it is then required that

(5.38)

\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\uparrow 0}\xi^{2}\theta^{\prime}(\xi)=\lim_{\xi\uparrow 0}\int_{0}^{\xi}\beta^{2}f(\theta(\beta))d\eta=0

or

(5.39)

\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\uparrow 0}\theta^{\prime}(\xi)=\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\lim_{\xi\uparrow 0}\int_{0}^{\xi}\beta^{2}f(\theta(\beta))d\beta=0

In terms of the supremum of
f(\theta)

(5.40)

\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\uparrow 0}\theta^{\prime}(\xi)\leq\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\lim_{\xi\uparrow 0}\int_{0}^{\xi}\beta^{2}\sup|f(\theta(\beta))|d\beta=\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\lim_{\xi\uparrow 0}\int_{0}^{\xi}\beta^{2}C|d\beta=\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\xi}\beta^{2}C=\lim_{\xi\uparrow 0}\frac{1}{3}C\xi=0

so that
\lim_{\xi\uparrow 0}\theta^{\prime}(\xi)=0
as required.

∎

Within the LE formalism, the total energy can also be expressed as

(5.41)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}=\frac{4-3\gamma}{\gamma-1}\int_{0}^{R}p(r)4\pi r^{2}dr

(5.42)

\displaystyle=\frac{4-3\gamma}{\gamma-1}4\pi\mathsf{K}\left(\frac{\mathsf{K}\gamma}{4\pi\mathscr{G}(\gamma-1)}\right)^{3/2}|\rho(0)|^{\frac{3\gamma-6}{2}}\int_{0}^{\xi_{1}}|\theta(\xi)|^{n+1}\xi^{2}d\xi

For
\gamma>6/5
, the solution vanishes at
\xi=\xi_{1}
so the radius of a polytropic star is

(5.43)

\displaystyle R=\left(\frac{4\pi\mathscr{G}(\gamma-1)}{\mathsf{K}\gamma)^{-1/2}}\right)|\rho(0)|^{(2-\gamma)/2}\xi

The mass is

\displaystyle M=\int_{0}^{R}4\pi r^{2}\rho(r)dr

\displaystyle=|4\pi\rho(0)|^{(3\gamma-4)/2}\left(\frac{\mathsf{K}\gamma}{4\pi\mathscr{G}(\gamma-1)}\right)^{3/2}\int_{0}^{\xi_{1}}\xi^{2}|\theta(\xi)|^{1/\gamma-1}d\xi

(5.44)

\displaystyle=|4\pi\rho(0)|^{(3\gamma-4)/2}\left(\frac{\mathsf{K}\gamma}{4\pi\mathscr{G}(\gamma-1)}\right)^{3/2}\xi_{1}^{2}\left|\frac{d\theta(\xi)}{d\xi}\right|_{\xi=\xi_{1}}

These formulas are useful in determining the masses and radii of white dwarf stars.

5.2.
Existence and uniqueness

Having formulated the Lane-Emden equations, it is necessary to establish that a solution exists and is unique. The following general theorem appears in
\bm{[14]}
.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
u(x)
be a positive solution to the general Lane-Emden equation

(5.45)

\displaystyle\Delta u+u^{n}=0,~~x\in\mathbf{R}^{N},N>2

If

(5.46)

\displaystyle 1\leq n<\frac{N+2}{N-2}

then
u(x)=0
. Otherwise there is a non-trivial positive solution.

The case N=2 is relevant to stellar structure which is our concern here, but for

n=(N+2)/(N-2)
and for
N=4
it is relevant to Yang Mills theory, while the case
N=2

is relevant to differential geometry. The coupled Lane-Emden system

\displaystyle\Delta u+v^{n}=0

\displaystyle\Delta v+u^{m}=0

also arises in many mathematical, biological, chemical and physical problems.
\bm{[15-20]}

We are really concerned with the existence and uniqueness of solutions of the LE equation in the spherically symmetric ’astrophysical form’ so that

(5.47)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\xi}\theta(\xi)=\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\frac{d}{d\xi}\left(\xi^{2}\frac{d\theta(\xi)}{d\xi}\right)\equiv\theta^{\prime\prime}(\xi)+\frac{2}{\xi}\theta^{\prime}(\xi)+|\theta(\xi)|^{n}=0,n>0

(5.48)

\displaystyle\theta(0)=\alpha,~~~\theta^{\prime}(0)=0

This equation can be considered as a special case of a general equation of the form

(5.49)

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime\prime}(\xi)+\mathcal{P}(\xi)\theta^{\prime}(\xi)+\mathcal{Q}(\theta(\xi),\xi)=0,~~\xi>0

(5.50)

\displaystyle\theta(0)=\alpha,~~\theta^{\prime}(0)=0

where
\mathcal{P}:\mathbf{R}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
,
\mathcal{Q}:\mathbf{R}\times\mathbf{R}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
,
\alpha>0
and
p(\xi)
can be singular at
\xi=0

\bm{[21]}
.

Proposition 5.9
.

The following general conditions are imposed on
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}

(1)

\mathcal{P}
is measurable on
[0,1]
,

(2)

\mathcal{P}\geq 0
on
(0,1]
.

(3)

\int_{0}^{1}s\mathcal{P}(s)ds<\infty

(4)

\mathcal{Q}
satisfies the general Caratheodry conditions and is bounded so that:

•

\exists,a,b
with
a<\alpha<\beta
and
C>0
such that
for all
t\in(0,1],\mathcal{Q}(\xi,\bullet)
is continuous on
(a,b)
.

•

For all
x\in(a,b)
,
\mathcal{Q}(\xi,\bullet)
is measurable on
(0,1]
.

•

\sup_{(t,x)\in[0,1]\times[a,b]}|\mathcal{Q}(\xi,x)|<C.

Definition 5.10
.

\theta(\xi)
is considered a solution to the LE equation iff
\exists~T>0
such that:

(1)

\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
are absolutely continuous on
(0,T]

(2)

\theta
satisfies (5.47) almost everywhere on
(0,T]
.

(3)

\theta
satisfies the conditions of (5.48).

Lemma 5.11
.

Given
\mathcal{P}(\xi)
, define a function
\psi(\xi)
by the integral

(5.51)

\displaystyle\psi(\xi)=\exp\left(\int_{1}^{\xi}\mathcal{P}(s)ds\right),~\xi>0

where
\psi(\xi)
is a nonnegative, nondecreasing and continuous function which is bounded
then the ODE (5.47) can be expressed in the form

(5.52)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\psi(\xi)}\frac{d}{d\xi}(\psi(\xi)\theta^{\prime}(\xi))=-\mathcal{Q}(\theta(\xi),\xi)

Proof.

Since

(5.53)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\xi)=\frac{\psi^{\prime}(\xi)}{\psi(\xi)}

then (-) becomes

(5.54)

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime\prime}(\xi)+\frac{\psi^{\prime}(\xi)}{\psi(\xi)}\theta^{\prime}(\xi)+\mathcal{Q}(\theta(\xi),\xi)=0,~~\xi>0

\displaystyle\theta(0)=\alpha,~~\theta^{\prime}(0)

(5.55)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\psi(t)}\big(\psi(\xi)\theta^{\prime\prime}(\xi)+\psi^{\prime}(\xi)\theta^{\prime}(\xi)\big)+\mathcal{Q}(\theta(\xi),\xi)=0

which is (5.52)
∎

The standard Lane-Emden equation is the case
\psi(\xi)=\xi^{-2}
.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let the conditions of Prop 4.9 be satisfied. Then a solution to the boundary value
problem (5.47) exists.

Proof.

The full proof is quite detailed and is given in
\bm{[21]}
.
∎

We will however, consider the uniqueness problem in detail. First two preliminary lemmas are required.

Lemma 5.13
.

The solution of the ODE

(5.56)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\psi(\xi)}\frac{d}{d\xi}(\psi(\xi)\theta^{\prime}_{i}(\xi))=-\mathcal{Q}(\theta_{i}(\xi),\xi),~i=1,2

has the integral representation

(5.57)

\displaystyle\theta_{i}(\xi)=\alpha-\int_{0}^{\xi}\left(\int_{s}^{\xi}\frac{\psi(s)}{\psi(\xi)}d\xi\right)\mathcal{Q}(\theta_{i}(s),s)ds

Proof.

From (-)

(5.58)

\displaystyle\psi(\xi)\frac{d\theta(\xi)}{d\xi}=-\int_{0}^{\xi}\psi(\xi)\mathcal{Q}(\xi,\theta_{i}(\xi))ds

so that

(5.59)

\displaystyle\frac{d\theta(\xi)}{d\xi}=-\frac{1}{\psi(\xi)}\int_{0}^{\xi}\psi(\xi)\mathcal{Q}(\xi,\theta_{i}(\xi))ds

Integrating once more for
\xi\in[0,T]
gives

\displaystyle\theta(\xi)=\alpha-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\psi(\xi)}\left(\int_{0}^{\xi}\psi(s)\mathcal{Q}(\xi,\theta(\xi))ds\right)d\xi

(5.60)

\displaystyle=\alpha-\int_{0}^{\xi}\left(\int_{s}^{\xi}\frac{\psi(s)}{\psi(\xi)}d\xi\right)\mathcal{Q}(\theta_{i}(s),s)ds

where the integration order has been changed.
∎

The next lemma is the basic Gronwall lemma

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
f:\mathbf{R}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
and let
C_{1},C_{2}>0
. Then if

(5.61)

\displaystyle f(x)\leq C_{1}+C_{2}\int_{I}f(\bar{x})d\bar{x}

where
I=[x_{0},x]
is an interval then one has the estimate or bound

(5.62)

\displaystyle f(x)\leq C_{1}\exp\left(C_{2}\int_{I}dx\right)=C_{1}\exp(C_{2}|x-x_{0}|)

and
f(x)\leq 0
if
C_{1}=0
. The result holds with
>
replacing
\leq
.

The main theorem on uniqueness of solutions can now be stated and proved.

Theorem 5.15
.

Let the conditions of definition (5.10) hold and let
\mathcal{Q}
be Lipschitz on
[a,b]
. Then the nonlinear elliptic boundary value problem (5.47) has a unique solution.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{Q}
is Lipschitz on
\theta(\xi)
on an interval
[a,b]
then
\exists
constant
\Lambda>0
such that

(5.63)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{Q}(\xi,\theta_{1}(\xi)-\mathcal{Q}(\xi_{2},\theta_{2}(\xi)|\leq\Lambda|\theta_{1}(\xi)-\theta_{2}(\xi)|

for all
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in(a,b)
and
\xi\in[0,1]
, where
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
are any two solutions to (5.47) on
[0,T]
. If
s\leq\xi\leq\zeta
then
\psi(s)/\psi(\xi)|\ll 1
. Now using the integral representation (5.57)

\displaystyle|\theta_{2}(\zeta)-\theta_{1}(\zeta)|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{\zeta}\left(\int_{s}^{\zeta}\frac{\psi(s)}{\psi(\xi)}d\xi\right)[\mathcal{Q}(\theta_{2}(s),s)-\mathcal{Q}(s,\theta(s))ds\right|

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\zeta}\left(\int_{s}^{\zeta}d\xi\right)\big|\mathcal{Q}(\theta_{2}(s),s)-\mathcal{Q}(\theta_{1}(s),s)\big|ds

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\zeta}\left(|\zeta-s|\right)\big|\mathcal{Q}(\theta_{2}(s),s)-\mathcal{Q}(\theta_{1}(s),s)\big|ds

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\int_{0}^{\zeta}\left(|\zeta-s|\right)\big|\theta_{2}(s)-\theta_{1}(s)\big|ds

(5.64)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\int_{0}^{\zeta}B\big|\theta_{2}(s)-\theta_{1}(s)\big|ds

Applying the Gronwall Lemma to

(5.65)

\displaystyle|\theta_{2}(\zeta)-\theta_{1}(\zeta)|\leq\int_{0}^{\zeta}C_{2}\big|\theta_{2}(s)-\theta_{1}(s)\big|ds

where
C_{2}=\lambda B
and
C_{1}=0
gives
|\theta_{2}(\zeta)-\theta_{1}(\zeta)|\leq 0

so that one must have
\theta_{1}(\zeta)=\theta_{2}(\zeta)
for any two solutions. Hence, the solution to the Lane-Emden nonlinear elliptic boundary value problem is unique.
∎

5.3.
Transformations of the LE equations and a dynamical systems perspective

The LE equations can be transformed to alternative forms which are useful for their solution
\bm{[1,12]}
. They can be recast into an autonomous 1st-order system of ODEs much like a dynamical system.

Lemma 5.16
.

The transformation
\theta=\chi/\xi
reduces the LE equations to

(5.66)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\xi}{d\xi^{2}}=-\frac{\chi^{n}}{\xi^{n-1}}

The Kelvin transform
x=1/\xi
reduces the LE equation to

(5.67)

\displaystyle x^{4}\frac{d{2}\theta}{dx^{2}}=-|\theta|^{n}

with the solution

(5.68)

\displaystyle\theta=\alpha x^{\eta},~~\eta=\frac{2}{n-1},~~\alpha=\left(\frac{2(n-3)}{(n-1)}\right)^{\frac{1}{n-1}},~~n>3

Proof.

It is easy to show that (5.66) is a solution of (5.65).
∎

Lemma 5.17
.

The substitution

(5.69)

\displaystyle\theta(x)=\beta x^{n}w(x)

transforms
x^{4}\frac{d{2}\theta}{dx^{2}}=-|\theta|^{n}
to the form

(5.70)

\displaystyle x^{2}\frac{d^{2}w}{dx^{2}}+2\eta x\frac{dw}{dx}+\eta(\eta-1)w+|w|^{n}=0

Proof.

The derivatives of (5.67) are

(5.71)

\displaystyle\frac{d\theta}{dx}=\beta x^{\eta}\frac{dw}{dx}+\eta\beta x^{\eta-1}w(x)

(5.72)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\theta}{dx^{2}}=\beta(x^{\eta}\frac{d^{2}w}{dx^{2}}+2\eta x^{\eta-1}\frac{dw}{dx}+\eta(\eta-1)x^{\eta-2}w

Substituting into (5.65) then gives (5.68)
∎

Lemma 5.18
.

Let

(5.73)

\displaystyle x=\frac{1}{\xi}=exp(w),~~w=\log x=-\log\xi,~~\lambda=1

then (5.68) becomes

(5.74)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}w}{dx^{2}}+\left(\frac{5-n}{n-1}\right)\frac{dw}{dz}-\frac{2(n-3)}{(n-1)^{2}}w+|w|^{n}=0

or

(5.75)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}w}{dx^{2}}=-2\Xi\left(w,\frac{dw}{dz}\right)

where

(5.76)

\displaystyle\Xi\left(w,\frac{dw}{dz}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(-\frac{n-5}{n-1}\right)\frac{dw}{dz}+\frac{2(3-n)}{(n-1)^{2}}w+|w|^{n}

Corollary 5.19
.

Equation (5.72) can be recast as a first-order system of autonomous ODES, much like a dynamical system so that

(5.77)

\displaystyle\frac{dw}{dz}=\mathcal{Q}

(5.78)

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathcal{Q}}{dz}=-2\Xi\left(w,\frac{dw}{dz}\right)\equiv-2\Xi\left(w,\mathcal{Q}\right)

and the critical points then lie along

(5.79)

\displaystyle\Xi(w,0)=0

These can be used to study stability. (REF)

5.4.
explicit solutions

The Lane-Emden system
LE_{n}
can be solved analytically
\bm{[1,2,12]}
for three values of
\gamma=1+\frac{1}{n}
, namely
n=0,1,5
.

•

If
n=0
or
\gamma=\infty
the LE equation is linear and homogenous with solution

\theta(\xi)=-\tfrac{1}{6}\xi^{2}
plus any linear combination of
\xi
and
1
. Then
LE_{0}
is

(5.80)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\frac{d}{d\xi}\left(\xi^{2}\frac{d\theta(\xi)}{d\xi}\right)+1=0

Rearranging and integrating once

(5.81)

\displaystyle\xi^{2}\frac{d\theta(\xi)}{d\xi}=c_{1}-\frac{1}{3}\xi^{3}

Diving through by
\xi^{2}
and integrating once more

(5.82)

\displaystyle\theta(\xi)=c_{o}-\frac{c_{1}}{\xi}-\frac{1}{6}\xi^{2}

The condition
\theta(0)=1
fixes the solution to be
\theta(\xi)=1-\tfrac{1}{6}\xi^{2}
with
\theta(0)=\sqrt{6}
and
\xi_{1}^{2}|d\theta(\xi)/d\xi|_{\xi=\xi_{1}}=-3\sqrt{6}
.
So
c_{0}=1,c_{1}=0
.

•

If
\gamma=2
then
n=1
so that

(5.83)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\frac{d}{d\xi}\left(\xi^{2}\frac{d\theta(\xi)}{d\xi}\right)+\theta=0

Using a power series ansatz
\theta(\xi)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}\xi^{n}
leads to recurrence relation
a_{n+2}=-a_{n}/(n+3)(n+2)
. The solution is

(5.84)

\displaystyle\theta(\xi)=\frac{a}{\xi}\sin\xi+\tfrac{b}{\xi}\cos\xi

The condition
\theta(0)=1
fixes the solution to be
\theta(\xi)=(\sin\xi)/\xi
. with
\xi_{1}=\pi
and
\xi_{1}^{2}|d\theta(\xi)/d\xi|_{\xi=\xi_{1}}=-\pi

•

If
\gamma=6/5
or
n=5
the solution is
\theta(\xi)=(1+\tfrac{1}{3}\xi^{2})^{-1/2}
with
\theta(\xi)\rightarrow 0
as
\xi\rightarrow\infty
so
\xi_{1}=\infty
and

\xi_{1}^{2}|d\theta(\xi)/d\xi|_{\xi=\xi_{1}}\rightarrow-\sqrt{3}
for
t\rightarrow\infty
. From (5.41) and (5.42) such polytropic stars have infinite radius but finite mass.
For
n=5
, equation (-) reduces to

(5.85)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}w}{dz^{2}}=\frac{1}{4}w(1-w^{4})

Multiply both sides by
dw/dz
so that

(5.86)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dz}\left(\left(\frac{dw}{dz}\right)^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{4}w(1-w^{4})\frac{dw}{dz}

Integrating

(5.87)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{dw}{dz}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int(w+w^{5})dw=\frac{1}{4}w^{2}-\frac{1}{12}w^{6}+C

then (choosing the negative so that
w\rightarrow\infty
)

(5.88)

\displaystyle\frac{dw}{w(1-\frac{1}{3}w^{4})^{1/2}}=-\frac{1}{2}dt

Now make the substitution

(5.89)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}w^{4}=\sin^{2}\zeta

First integrate it so that

(5.90)

\displaystyle\frac{4}{3}w^{3}dw=2\sin\zeta\cos\zeta d\zeta

then it follows that

(5.91)

\displaystyle\frac{dw}{w}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\cos\zeta}{\sin\zeta}d\zeta

from (5.88) and (5.89) we obtain

(5.92)

\displaystyle cosec\zeta d\zeta=-dz

Integrating

(5.93)

\displaystyle\int cosec\zeta d\zeta=\log[\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\zeta)]-\log[\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}\zeta)]=\log[\tan(\frac{1}{2}\zeta]=-\int dz

so

(5.94)

\displaystyle\tan(\frac{1}{2}\zeta)=C\exp(-z)

Then

(5.95)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}w^{4}=\frac{4\tan^{2}(\frac{1}{2}\zeta)}{1+\tan^{2}(\frac{1}{2}\zeta))^{2}}

which is

(5.96)

\displaystyle w=\left(\frac{12\tan^{2}(\frac{1}{2}\zeta)}{1+\tan^{2}(\frac{1}{2}\zeta))^{2}}\right)^{1/4}

Using (5.92)

(5.97)

\displaystyle w=\left(\frac{12C^{2}\exp(-2z)}{1+C^{2}\exp(-2z))^{2}}\right)^{1/4}

then

(5.98)

\displaystyle\theta(\xi)=\left(\frac{3C^{2}}{1+C^{2}\xi^{2})^{2}}\right)^{1/4}

Since
\theta(-)=1
it follows that
C^{2}=1/3
so the solution is

(5.99)

\displaystyle\theta(\theta)=\left(\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{3}\xi^{2}}\right)^{1/4}

•

Other values of n or
\gamma
, also the most interesting and astro-physically relevant, require numerical solutions. The case
\tfrac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
or
3<\gamma<2
can model neutron stars. The case
\gamma=5/3
or
n=3/2
describes stars with convective cores such as red giants and brown dwarfs and stars with perfect gas compositions.

•

The case
\gamma=1
or
n=\infty
corresponds to an isothermal gas sphere and will be considered separately.

5.5.
The isothermal gas sphere again

The isothermal gas sphere was previously discussed. Here, it is reprised again for the case of a polytropic gas and can be described by a Lane-Emden type equation known as the Chandrasekhar equation
\bm{[1]}
.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let a spherical region
\mathbf{B}(0,R)
of radius R, centre zero, support a polytropic gas
with pressure
p(r)
and density
\rho(r)
and polytropic index
\gamma=1
or
n=\infty
. Then
p=\mathsf{K}|\rho|^{\gamma}=\mathsf{K}|\rho|^{1+\frac{1}{n}}
. The (Poisson)equation of hydrostatic equilibrium of the isothermal sphere is then given by (2.80) and (2.81) as

(5.100)

\displaystyle\Delta\log(\rho)=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}\frac{d}{dr}(\log\rho)\right)=-\frac{4\pi\mathscr{G}\rho(r)}{\mathsf{K}}

or equivalently

(5.101)

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi(r)=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}\frac{d\Phi(r)}{dr}\right)=4\pi\mathscr{G}\rho_{c}\exp\left(-\frac{\Phi(r)}{\mathsf{K}}\right)

and there is a singular solution

(5.102)

\displaystyle\rho(r)=\frac{A}{2\pi\mathscr{G}r^{2}}

Using the substitutions

(5.103)

\displaystyle\rho=\lambda e^{-\psi},~~r=a\xi=\left|\frac{A}{4\pi G\lambda}\right|^{1/2}\xi

where
\lambda\in\mathbf{R}
, then the ODE (5.98) reduces to the dimensionless Chandrasekhar equation

(5.104)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\frac{d}{d\xi}\left(\xi^{2}\frac{d\psi(\xi)}{d\xi}\right)=e^{-\psi(\xi)}

which is the Lane-Emden equation for
\gamma=1
or
n=\infty
. One can choose
\lambda=\rho_{c}
with
\lim_{\xi\rightarrow 0}\frac{d\psi(0)}{d\xi}=0
if
\psi(\xi)
bounded. The boundary conditions are
\psi(0)=0,\tfrac{d\psi(\xi)}{d\xi}=0,\xi=0
. The analysis of the CE is then similar to that of the LE equation.
The CE can be transformed in various ways
\bm{[1]}
using

(5.105)

\displaystyle X=\frac{1}{\lambda}

then the CE becomes

(5.106)

\displaystyle X^{4}\frac{d^{2}\psi(X)}{dX^{2}}=\exp(-\psi(X))

which has a solution

(5.107)

\displaystyle\psi(X)=-\log 2-2\log X

The Emden transform then introduces a variable Z

(5.108)

\displaystyle Z=-\psi-2\log X

so that

(5.109)

\displaystyle\frac{d\psi(X)}{dX}=-\frac{2}{X}-\frac{dZ}{dX}

(5.110)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\psi(X)}{dX^{2}}=-\frac{2}{X}^{2}-\frac{d^{2}Z}{dX^{2}}

(5.111)

\displaystyle X^{2}\frac{d^{2}Z}{dX^{2}}=e^{Z}-2=0

To eliminate
X
set
X=1/\xi=e^{\beta}
then

(5.112)

\displaystyle\frac{dZ}{d\beta}=e^{-\beta}\frac{dZ}{d\beta}

(5.113)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}Z}{d\beta^{2}}=e^{-2\beta}\left(\frac{d^{2}Z}{d\beta^{2}}-\frac{dZ}{d\beta}\right)

giving

(5.114)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}Z}{d\beta^{2}}-\frac{dZ}{d\beta}+e^{Z}-2=0

6.
The Fundamental Hydrostatic Equilibrium Equation (FHEE) And The Euler-Poisson Equations For A Self-Gravitating Gaseous Star

The previous derivation and applications of the FEHE were mostly heuristic. In this section, the FEHE is now considered and derived from a more formal mathematical perspective. The fundamental description of a self-gravitating gaseous star are the Euler-Poisson system of nonlinear partial differential equations for a self-gravitating gas/fluid. These are essentially the Euler equations of fluid dynamics (with viscosity set to zero) coupled to a Newtonian gravitational potential. The Euler-Poisson system has been studied extensively in many works. (For example
\bm{[22-26]}
and references their in.)

Definition 6.1
.

The scenario describing a self-gravitating gas/fluid described by the Euler-Poisson equations is defined as follows.

(1)

Let
\mathbf{D}\subset\mathbf{R}^{3}
. Generally, we use the functions

\rho:[0,T)\times\mathbf{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}_{\geq 0},p:[0,T)\times\mathbf{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\Phi:[0,T)\times\mathbf{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
for some temporal interval
[0,T)
, to describe the spatio-temporal distributions of mass density, pressure and Newtonian potential of the gas/fluid. Then
\rho=\rho(\mathbf{x},t),p=p(\mathbf{x},t),\Phi=\Phi(\mathbf{x},t)
.

(2)

The self-gravitating gas is supported within a spherical region
\mathbf{D}(\lambda(t))\equiv\mathbf{D}(t)
defined by

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}(\lambda(t))=\mathbf{D}(t)=supp~\rho(t,\bullet)=\{(\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{R}^{3}|\rho(\mathbf{x},t)>0,\lambda(t)\gtrless\lambda(0)=R\}\subset\mathbf{R}^{3}

with varying radius
\lambda(t)\gtrless\lambda(0)=R
depending on whether the self-gravitating gaseous sphere is collapsing or expanding, or
\lambda(t)=\lambda(0)=R
in hydrostatic equilibrium. Then
\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{D}(t))=\tfrac{4}{3}\pi|\lambda(t)|^{3}
is the volume at time t.

(3)

The (moving) boundary is
\partial\mathbf{D}(t)\subset\mathbf{D}(t)
with surface area
\mathrm{A}(\mathbf{D}(t))=4\pi|\lambda(t)|^{2}
. The boundary conditions are that the pressure and density vanish here so that
\rho(\mathbf{x},t)=p(\mathbf{x},t)=0
for all
x\in\partial\mathbf{D}(t)
. If
\mathbf{D}(t)\bigcup\mathbf{D}^{c}(t)=\mathbf{R}^{3}
then the vacuum region is defined by
\rho(\mathbf{x},t)=0
in
\mathbf{D}^{c}(t)
and the matter or hydrodynamic region by
\rho(\mathbf{x},t)>0,x\in\mathbf{D}(t)
. The fluid velocity
\mathbf{u}
is defined only on
\mathbf{D}(t)
by
\mathbf{u}\equiv u^{i}:[0,T)\times\mathbf{D}(t)\rightarrow\mathbf{R}^{3}
and there is no definition in
\mathbf{D}^{c}(t)
. The space
\mathbf{D}(0)
is taken to be precompact. A diffuse boundary condition can also be defined as (ref)

(6.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{(\mathbf{x},t)\rightarrow(\overline{\mathbf{x}},\overline{t})}\rho(\mathbf{x},t)\nabla_{i}p(\mathbf{x},t))=0

where
(x,t)\in[0,T)\times\mathbf{D}(t)
and
(\overline{x},\overline{t})\in[0,T)\times\partial\mathbf{D}(t)
, where again
\partial\mathbf{D}(t)
is the boundary of the volume
\mathbf{D}(t)
, that is, the moving interface between the fluid/gas matter and the exterior vacuum. This boundary condition characterizes the gradually vanishing fluid pressure per mass near the boundary.

(4)

The Newtonian potential
\Phi
exists on
\mathbf{R}^{3}=\mathbf{D}(t)\bigcup\mathbf{D}^{c}(t)
, in both the matter and vacuum regions and obeys the Poisson equation

(6.2)

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi=4\pi\mathscr{G}\rho,~~~in~~\mathbf{R}^{3}

with the general solution

(6.3)

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{x},t)=-\mathscr{G}{\int}_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(\mathbf{y},t)d^{3}\mathbf{y}}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|},~~~~for~(t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in[0,T)\times\mathbf{R}^{3}

(5)

It is generally assumed that the gas is
isentropic
having constant entropy or entropy per nucleon, and obeys a barytropic pressure law of the form
p=p(\rho)
, typically a polytropic gas law

(6.4)

\displaystyle p=\mathsf{K}|\rho|^{\gamma},~~\mathsf{x}\in\mathbf{D}(t)\in\mathbf{R}^{3}

(Polytropic stars will be considered in detail in Section (5) The set
\{\rho(x,t),p(x,t),\Phi(x,t)\}
is also a solution to the Euler-Poisson system of equations but this will be considered formally in Section (6). For a gas in hydrostatic equilibrium
\mathbf{u}
=0 and
\rho:\mathbf{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}_{\geq 0},p:\mathbf{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\Phi:\mathbf{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
so that

\rho=\rho(\mathbf{x}),p=p(\mathbf{x}),\Phi=\Phi(\mathbf{x})
. The volume of the support is now fixed so that
\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{D}(t))\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{D}(0))=\tfrac{4}{3}\pi R^{3}\equiv\tfrac{4}{3}\pi|\lambda(0)|^{3}
.

(6)

For self-gravitating fluid-gas systems like stars in equilibrium at radius
R
, it is also natural to assume spherical symmetry and coordinates so that
\rho=\rho(r),p=p(r),\Phi=\Phi(r)
with central values
\rho(0),p(0)
and boundary values
\rho(R)=p(R)=0
. The domain
\mathbf{D}(\lambda(t))=\mathbf{D}(\lambda(0))\equiv\mathbf{D}(0)=\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{x}_{o},R)
is now a static ball with centre
\mathbf{x}_{o}
an radius
R
. Then

(6.5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}(\lambda(0)=R)=\mathbf{B}(0,R)=\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{R}^{3}|d(x,y)\leq R\}

The mass contained within a radius
r
is or ball
\mathbf{B}(0,r)\subset\mathbf{B}(0,R)
is then
\mathcal{M}(r)
and
\mathcal{M}(R)=M
, the total mass contained within
\mathbf{B}(0,R)
It is defined as

(6.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(r)=\int_{\mathbf{B}(0,r)}4\pi\widehat{r}^{2}\rho(\widehat{r})d{r}\equiv\int_{0}^{r}4\pi\widehat{r}^{2}\rho(\widehat{r})d\overline{r}

Definition 6.2
.

If the fluid/gas is described by a fundamental Boltzmann equation then the continuity of mass and Navier-Stokes equations can be established as a consequence. In the absence of gravitation they are

(6.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\nabla.(\rho\mathbf{u})=0~~~~x\in\mathbf{D}(t),t>0

(6.8)

\displaystyle\rho(\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}+(\bm{u}.\nabla)\bm{u})+\nabla p=\eta\Delta\bm{u}~~~~x\in\mathbf{D}(t),t>0

and with the following boundary and initial conditions.

(6.9)

\displaystyle P=\rho=0~on~\partial\mathbf{D}

(6.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\partial\mathbf{D}}=\bm{u}.\bm{n}(t)~on~\partial\mathbf{D}

(6.11)

\displaystyle(\rho(0,\bullet),\bm{u}(0,\bullet))=(\rho_{o},u_{o}),~in~\mathbf{D}(t)

where
\mathcal{U}_{\partial\mathbf{D}}
is the normal velocity of the moving domain boundary
\partial\mathbf{D}(t)
with unit normal
\bm{n}(t)
.
If the gas is now coupled to Newtonian gravitation and becomes a self-gravitating gas then one obtains the Euler-Poisson system system of nonlinear PDEs.

(6.12)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\nabla.(\rho\mathbf{u})=0,~~x\in\mathbf{D}(t),t>0

(6.13)

\displaystyle\rho(\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}+(\bm{u}.\nabla)\bm{u})+\nabla p=-\rho\nabla{\Phi},~~~~x\in\mathbf{D}(t),t>0

(6.14)

\displaystyle\Delta{\Phi}=4\pi\mathscr{G}\rho,~~~~x\in\mathbf{D}(t),t>0

(6.15)

\displaystyle p=\rho=0~on~\partial\mathbf{D}

(6.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\partial\mathbf{D}}=\bm{u}.\bm{n}(t)~on~\partial\mathbf{D}

(6.17)

\displaystyle(\rho(0,\bullet),\bm{u}(0,\bullet))=(\rho_{o},u_{o}),~in~\mathbf{D}(t)

and the gas-vacuum boundary is
\mathbf{R}^{3}\char 92\relax\partial\mathbf{D}
.
One can also introduce a mass function
\mathcal{M}(r,t)

(6.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\rho)=\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(x,t)d\mathrm{V}(x)

where
\int_{\mathbf{B}(t)}d\mathrm{V}(x)=\int_{\mathbf{B}(t)}d^{n}x=\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{B}(t))
.

The standard notations apply so that

(6.19)

\displaystyle\nabla.(\rho\bm{u})=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}(\rho u^{k})\equiv\sum_{k=1}^{3}\nabla_{k}(\rho u^{k}),j,k=1,2,3

(6.20)

\displaystyle(\bm{u}.\nabla)\bm{u}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}u^{k}\frac{\partial u^{j}}{\partial x^{k}}\equiv\sum_{k=1}^{3}u^{k}\nabla_{k}u^{j}

(6.21)

\displaystyle\nabla Z=(\nabla_{1}Z,\nabla_{2}Z,\nabla_{3}Z),~Z=p,\mathscr{B}

(6.22)

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{k}\partial x^{k}}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\partial_{k}\partial^{k}\Phi\equiv\nabla^{2}

The EL system can also be expressed in various coordinate systems
\bm{[22]}
. One can also use Lagrangian derivatives
D/Dt
whereby

(6.23)

\displaystyle\frac{D}{Dt}=\partial_{t}+\bm{u}.\nabla\equiv\partial_{t}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}u^{k}\nabla_{k}

so that (6.14) and (6.15) become

(6.24)

\displaystyle\frac{D\rho}{Dt}+\rho\nabla.\bm{u}=0

(6.25)

\displaystyle\rho\frac{Dv}{Dt}+\nabla p=-\rho\nabla\Phi

The EL system of PDEs can be closed by an equation of state
p=p(\rho)
with
dp/d\rho>0
and the polytropic equation can be utilised so that
p=\mathsf{K}|\rho|^{\gamma}
with
\gamma\in[1,2]
. We also reprise the enthalpy variable
\mathfrak{E}(\rho)
and the functional
\psi(\rho)
so that
\mathfrak{E}(\rho)=\int_{0}^{\rho}dp/\rho
and
\psi(\rho)=\int_{0}^{\rho}\mathfrak{E}(\rho)d\rho
. The notation
EP_{\gamma}
will denote an Euler-Poisson system with a polytropic gas with index
\gamma
.

Definition 6.3
.

A solution
\rho=\rho(x,t),\phi=\phi(x,t)
is a compactly supported classical solution (CSCS) iff
\forall(\rho,u,\phi)\in C^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbf{R}^{2}),\phi(t,\bullet)\in C^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{2}),\rho>0
,everywhere and the support of
\rho(t,\bullet)
is compact in
\mathbf{D}(t)
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

For any CSCS the solution of the Poisson equation (6.14) is the Newtonian potential

(6.26)

\displaystyle\phi(x,t)=-\mathscr{G}\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho\frac{(x^{\prime},t)}{|x-x^{\prime}|}d\mathbf{V}(x^{\prime})

The EP equations have been studied quite extensively and there are expanding and collapsing solutions.

Definition 6.4
.

The total mass
\mathcal{M}(\rho)
was defined. The total energy at any time
t>0
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}(\rho,\bm{u})=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}(x)+\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\psi(\rho)d\mathrm{V}(x)+\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\frac{1}{2}\rho\phi d\mathrm{V}(x)

(6.27)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}(x)+\psi(\rho)\right)d\mathrm{V}(x)-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{G}\int\int\frac{\rho(x,t)\rho(x^{\prime},t)}{|x-x^{\prime}|}d\mathrm{V}(x)d\mathrm{V}(x^{\prime}

For a polytropic gas

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}(\rho,\bm{u})=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}(x)+\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\frac{\mathsf{K}}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma}d\mathrm{V}(x)+\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\frac{1}{2}\rho\phi d\mathrm{V}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}(x)+\frac{\mathsf{K}}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma}\right)d\mathrm{V}(x)-\frac{\mathscr{G}}{2}\int\int\frac{\rho(x,t)\rho(x^{\prime},t)}{|x-x^{\prime}|}d\mathrm{V}(x)d\mu(x^{\prime}

(6.28)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}d\mu(x)+\frac{p}{\gamma-1}\right)d\mathrm{V}(x)-\frac{\mathscr{G}}{2}\int\!\!\!\int\frac{\rho(x,t)\rho(x^{\prime},t)}{|x-x^{\prime}|}d\mu(x)d\mathrm{V}(x^{\prime})

Theorem 6.5
.

The total energy
\mathbf{E}(\rho,\bm{u})
is conserved for any solution
(\rho,\bm{u})
so that

(6.29)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{E}(\rho,\bm{u})=0

Proof.

(6.30)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{E}(\rho,\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\int\frac{1}{2}\rho\bm{u}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}=\frac{1}{2}\int\partial_{t}(\rho\sum_{k}|u_{k}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}

\displaystyle=\int\rho\sum_{k}u^{k}\partial_{t}u^{k}d\mathrm{V}+\int\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\rho\sum_{k}|u_{k}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}

\displaystyle=-\int(\frac{1}{2}\rho\sum_{j,k}u^{j}\nabla)_{j}|u^{k}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}+\int\sum_{k}u^{k}\nabla_{k}pd\mathrm{V}

\displaystyle+\int\sum_{k}\rho u^{k}\nabla_{k}\Phi d\mathrm{V}+\int\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{j}|\rho u^{j}|\sum_{k}|u_{k}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}

\displaystyle=-\int(\frac{1}{2}\rho\sum_{j,k}|u^{j}\nabla_{j}|u^{k}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}+\int\sum_{k}u^{k}\nabla_{k}pd\mathrm{V}

\displaystyle+\int\sum_{k}\rho u^{k}\nabla_{k}\Phi+\int\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{j}|\rho u^{j})\sum_{k}|u^{k}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}

\displaystyle=-\underbrace{\int\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,k}\nabla_{j}(\rho u^{j}|u^{k}|^{2})-\int\sum_{k}u^{k}(\nabla_{k}p+\rho\nabla_{k}\Phi)d\mathrm{V}}

\displaystyle=-\int\sum_{k}\rho u^{k}\nabla_{k}\mathfrak{E}d\mathrm{V}+\int\sum_{k}\rho u^{k}\nabla_{k}\Phi d\mathrm{V}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial t}d\mathrm{V}+\int\underbrace{\sum_{k}\nabla_{k}(\rho u^{k})}_{use~EP~eqn~again}\Phi d\mathrm{V}

(6.31)

\displaystyle=-\frac{d}{dt}\int\Psi d\mathrm{V}-\int\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}\Phi d\mathrm{V}

The last term can be re-expressed as

\displaystyle\int\partial_{t}\rho\Phi d\mathrm{V}

\displaystyle=-\mathscr{G}\int\!\!\!\int\frac{\partial_{t}\rho(x,t)\rho(x,t)}{|x-x^{\prime}|}d\mathrm{V}(x)d\mathrm{V}(x^{\prime})

(6.32)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int\!\!\!\int\frac{\rho(x,t)\rho(x,t)}{|x-x^{\prime}|}d\mathrm{V}(x)d\mathrm{V}(x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int\rho\Phi d\mathrm{V}

Hence

(6.33)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int\frac{1}{2}\rho|u|^{2}d\mathrm{V}+\frac{d}{dt}\int\Psi d\mathrm{V}+\frac{d}{dt}\int\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi d\mathrm{V}=0

and so the total energy is conserved along t for any compact supported solution
(\rho,\bm{u})
,
∎

Solutions of an
EP_{\gamma}
system have a self-similar re-scaling property under

\overline{t}=t/\lambda^{1/(2-\gamma)}
and
\overline{x}=x/\lambda
. If
(\rho,\bm{u})
is a solution of an
EP_{\gamma}
system then so is the pair
(\overline{\rho},\overline{\bm{u}})
given by
\bm{[32,36]}
.

(6.34)

\displaystyle{\rho(x,t)}=\lambda^{-\frac{2}{2-\gamma}}\overline{\rho}(\frac{t}{\lambda^{\frac{1}{2-\gamma}}},\frac{x}{\lambda})

(6.35)

\displaystyle{\bm{u}(x,t)}=\lambda^{-\frac{\gamma-1}{2-\gamma}}\overline{\bm{u}}(\frac{t}{\lambda^{\frac{1}{2-\gamma}}},\frac{x}{\lambda})

Also, given a solution
(p,\phi)
for the pressure and Newtonian potential, the rescaled pressure and potential are

(6.36)

\displaystyle{p(x,t)}=\lambda^{-\frac{2\gamma}{2-\gamma}}\overline{o}(\frac{t}{\lambda^{\frac{1}{2-\gamma}}},\frac{x}{\lambda})

(6.37)

\displaystyle{\phi(x,t)}=\lambda^{-\frac{2\gamma-2}{2-\gamma}}\overline{\phi}(\frac{t}{\lambda^{\frac{1}{2-\gamma}}},\frac{x}{\lambda})

The mass
M(\rho)
and the energy
E(\rho,u)
are rescaled as

(6.38)

\displaystyle M(\rho)=\lambda^{\frac{4-3\gamma}{2-\gamma}}\overline{M(\overline{\rho})}

(6.39)

\displaystyle E(\rho,\bm{u})=\lambda^{\frac{6-5\gamma}{2-\gamma}}\overline{E(\overline{\rho},\overline{\bm{u}})}

The mass is invariant under a re-scaling when
\gamma=4/3
and the energy when
\gamma=5/6
.
so the re-scalings are respectively mass-critical and energy critical.

The
EP_{\gamma}
system applied to a spherical gaseous star is naturally best expressed in spherical symmetry then
r=\|x\|
. Define a ball
\mathbf{B}(\lambda(t),0)
, centred at the origin, then the ’radius of support of the fluid’ is
\lambda(t)
at time
t
. Then

u=u(r,t),\rho=\rho(r,t),p=p(r,t),\phi=\phi(r,t)
. The
EL_{\gamma}
system of PDE are then

(6.40)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p+u\partial_{r}\rho+\rho\partial_{r}u+\frac{2}{r}\rho u=0,~r\leq\lambda(t)

(6.41)

\displaystyle\rho(\partial_{t}u+u\partial_{r}u)+\partial_{r}p=-\rho\partial_{r}\Phi~r\leq\lambda(t)

(6.42)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}(r^{2}\partial_{r}\Phi)=4\pi\mathscr{G}\rho,~r\leq\lambda(t)

(6.43)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\Phi(r,t)=0

(6.44)

\displaystyle p(t,\xi(t))=0

(6.45)

\displaystyle\mathrm{V}_{\partial\mathbf{B}}=\dot{\lambda}(t)=\mathrm{V}(t,\lambda(t))

(6.46)

\displaystyle\lambda(0)=\lambda_{o},\rho(0,r)=\rho_{0}(r),u(0,r)=u_{o}(r)

Corollary 6.6
.

For a static or equilibrium solution of the
EP
system one must have
\bm{u}=0
and

\partial_{k}\bm{u}=\partial_{t}\bm{u}=0
. The spherically symmetric
EP
system then reduces to

(6.47)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}p(r)=-\rho(r)\partial_{r}\Phi(r),~~r\leq R\equiv\lambda

(6.48)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}(\partial_{r}(r^{2}\partial_{r}\Phi(r))=4\pi\mathscr{G}\rho(r)

or

(6.49)

\displaystyle\frac{dp(r)}{dr}=-\rho(r)\frac{d\Phi(r)}{dr}

(6.50)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}\frac{d\Phi(r)}{dr}\right)=4\pi\mathscr{G}\rho(r)

Writing

(6.51)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}\frac{d\Phi(r)}{dr}\right)=4\pi r^{2}\mathscr{G}\rho(r)

then integrating

(6.52)

\displaystyle r^{2}\frac{d\Phi(r)}{dr}=\int_{0}^{r}4\pi\mathscr{G}r^{\prime 2}\rho(r^{\prime})dr^{\prime}

Now using (6.49)

(6.53)

\displaystyle\frac{d\Phi(r)}{dr}=-\frac{1}{\rho(r)}\frac{dp(r)}{dr}=\frac{\int_{0}^{r}4\pi\mathscr{G}r^{\prime 2}\rho(r^{\prime})dr^{\prime}}{r^{2}}\equiv\frac{\mathcal{M}(r)}{r^{2}}

and so using (6.51) the FEHE is recovered once more

\displaystyle\frac{dp(r)}{dr}=-\frac{\mathscr{G}\mathcal{M}(r)\rho(r)}{r^{2}}

Lemma 6.7
.

For an
EP_{\gamma}
system, the energy equation (6.28) is equivalent to (2.68) with

\mathbf{E}_{K}=0
for equilibrium.

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}(\rho,\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}+\int\Psi(\rho)d\mathrm{V}+\int\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi d\mathrm{V}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}+\int\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}d\mathrm{V}+\int\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi d\mathrm{V}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}+\int\mathcal{E}d\mathrm{V}+\int\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi d\mathrm{V}

(6.54)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{1}{2}\rho(r)|\bm{u}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}+\int\mathcal{E}(r)d\mathrm{V}-\int\rho\frac{\mathscr{G}\mathcal{M}(r)}{r}d\mathrm{V}

In equilibrium the ’kinetic energy’ term is zero so that
\mathbf{E}_{K}=0.
Continuing in spherical symmetry

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}(\rho,0)

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{E}(r)d\mathrm{V}-\int\rho\frac{\mathscr{G}\mathcal{M}(r)}{r}d\mathrm{V}

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{E}(r)4\pi r^{3}dr-\int\rho\frac{\mathscr{G}\mathcal{M}(r)}{r}4\pi r^{2}dr

(6.55)

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{E}(r)4\pi r^{3}dr-\int\frac{\mathscr{G}\mathcal{M}(r)d\mathcal{M}(r)}{r}=\mathbb{E}_{T}+\mathbb{E}_{G}

∎

Proposition 6.8
.

Let an EP system of self-gravitating gas/fluid with compact support on some

\mathbf{D}(t)
have total energy
E(\rho,\bm{u})
, mass
M(\rho)
, density
\rho=\rho(x,t)
, pressure
p=p(x,t)
and Newtonian potential
\Phi=\Phi(x,t)
. Let
Q(\rho,\bm{u})=\tfrac{d}{dt}E(\rho,\bm{u})
. The enthalpy variable
\mathfrak{E}(\rho)
is

\mathfrak{E}(\rho)=\int_{0}^{\rho}dp/\rho
and
\psi(\rho)=\int_{0}^{\rho}\mathfrak{E}(\rho)d\rho
with
barytropic state equation
p=p(\rho)
. Then the total energy is conserved if

\mathbf{E}(\rho,\bm{u})=\tfrac{d}{dt}\mathbf{E}(\rho,\bm{u})=0
. If the total energy is conserved

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{E}(\rho,\bm{u})=\frac{d}{dt}\left|\int\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}+\psi(\rho)+\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi)d\mathrm{V}(x)\right|

(6.56)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left|\int\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}+\psi(\rho)-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{G}\int\int\frac{\rho(x,t)\rho(x^{\prime},t)}{|x-x^{\prime}}|d\mathrm{V}(x)d\mathrm{V}(x^{\prime})\right|=0

Then the hydrostatic equilibrium equation

(6.57)

\nabla_{k}p+\rho\nabla_{k}\Phi=0

holds iff
u=0
.

Proof.

Hence
\tfrac{d}{dt}\mathbf{E}(\rho,\bm{u}=0
and the total energy is conserved. Returning now to the underbraced term in (6.33) this constraint implies that

(6.58)

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathbf{E}(\rho,\bm{u})}{dt}=\frac{d}{dt}\int\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}+\int\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,k}\nabla_{j}[\rho u^{j}|u^{k}|^{2})d\mathrm{V}+\int\sum_{k}u^{k}[\nabla_{k}p+\rho\nabla_{k}\Phi]d\mathrm{V}=0

Suppose the term involving the gradients of the pressure and gravitational potential vanishes then

(6.59)

\displaystyle\sum_{k}(\nabla_{k}p+\rho\nabla_{k}\Phi)=0

Then one must

(6.60)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}=-\int\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,k}\nabla_{j}[\rho u^{j}|u^{k}|^{2})d\mathrm{V}

or

(6.61)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int(\dot{\rho}|u|^{2}+2\rho{u}_{k}\dot{{u}}^{k})d\mathrm{V}=-\int\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,k}\nabla_{j}[\rho u^{j}|u^{k}|^{2})d\mathrm{V}

This can only be satisfied for
\bm{u}=0
and
\dot{\rho}=0
, that is for static equilibrium. In spherical symmetry the FEHE becomes
\partial_{r}p(r)+\rho\partial_{r}\Phi=0
or

(6.62)

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{dp(r)}{dr}=-\rho(r)\frac{d\Phi(r)}{dr}=-\frac{\mathscr{G}\rho(r)\mathcal{M}(r)}{r^{2}}}

which is again the FEHE.
∎

Proposition 6.9
.

The conservation of total energy within
\mathbf{D}(t)
is equivalent to the 1st variation with respect to the density so that

(6.63)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbf{E}}(t)\delta t=\delta\mathbf{E}(t)=\frac{\delta\mathbf{E}}{\delta\rho}\delta\rho=0

In full

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}+\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi\right)d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})=\delta\left|\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}+\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi\right)d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})\right|

(6.64)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{d\rho}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}+\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi\right)d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})\delta\rho=0

If
\mathbf{u}=0
or
\mathbf{u}=const
with
\delta_{i}\mathbf{u}=0
then

(6.65)

\displaystyle\delta\left|\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}+\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi\right)d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})\right|

(6.66)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}p+\rho\nabla_{i}\Phi=0

which is the FE of hydrostatic equilibrium. Then in spherical symmetry we once
again recover the basic stellar structure equation

(6.67)

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{dp(r)}{dr}=-\frac{\mathscr{G}\mathcal{M}(r)\rho(r)}{r^{2}}}

Proof.

\displaystyle\delta\mathbf{E}

\displaystyle=\delta\left|\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}+\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi\right)d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left|\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}+\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi\right)d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})\right|dt

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left|\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}+\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi\right)d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})\right|\frac{dt}{d\rho}\delta\rho

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{d\rho}\frac{d\rho}{dt}\left|\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}+\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi\right)d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})\right|\frac{dt}{d\rho}\delta\rho

(6.68)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{d\rho}\left|\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}+\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi\right)d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})\right|\delta\rho=0

Now

(6.69)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbf{D(t)}}\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})dt\equiv\frac{d}{d\rho}\int_{\mathbf{D(t)}}\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})d\rho=\int_{\mathbf{D(t)}}u_{i}(\nabla_{i}p)d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})\delta\rho

(6.70)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbf{D(t)}}\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})dt\equiv\frac{d}{d\rho}\int_{\mathbf{D(t)}}\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})d\rho=\int_{\mathbf{D}}u_{i}\rho(\nabla_{i}\Phi)d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})\delta\rho

and since

(6.71)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\rho}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})\right)\delta\rho\equiv\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{d\rho}\rho|\bm{u}|^{2}\right)d\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{x})\delta\rho

we have

\displaystyle\delta\mathrm{E}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{d}{d\rho}\big(\rho|u_{i}u^{i}|\big)+u_{i}\big(\nabla_{i}p+\rho\nabla_{i}\Phi\big)\right)d\mathrm{V}(x)\delta\rho

(6.72)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(u^{i}u_{i}+2\rho u_{i}\frac{du_{i}}{d\rho}+u_{i}\big(\nabla_{i}p+\rho\nabla_{i}\Phi\big)\right)d\mathrm{V}(x)\delta\rho=0

assuming
du^{i}/d\rho\neq 0
. However,
\tfrac{du^{i}}{d\rho}=\tfrac{du^{i}}{dt}\tfrac{dt}{d\rho}=0
if
u^{i}=const
or
\rho(\mathbf{x},t)=\rho(\mathbf{x})
. Then

(6.73)

\displaystyle u^{i}u_{i}+2\rho u_{i}\frac{du_{i}}{d\rho}+u_{i}\big(\nabla_{i}p+\rho\nabla_{i}\Phi\big)=0

which is

(6.74)

\displaystyle u^{i}+2\rho\frac{du_{i}}{d\rho}+\big(\nabla_{i}p+\rho\nabla_{i}\Phi\big)

For static or equilibrium configurations
u^{i}=0
so that (6.76) reduces to

(6.75)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}p+\rho\nabla_{i}\Phi=0

which is again the fundamental equation of hydrostatic equilibrium. In spherical symmetry

(6.76)

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{dp(r)}{dr}=-\rho(r)\frac{d\Phi(r)}{dr}=-\frac{\rho(r)\mathscr{G}\mathcal{M}(r)}{r^{2}}}

∎

As before, this can be reduced to the Lane-Emden ODE if one utilises a polytropic gas equation of state, and the results of Section 2 then apply. Of particular interest is the mass- critical case
\gamma=4/3
, discussed in Section and now reconsidered. A polytropic gas with
\gamma=4/3
is essentially radiation. In supermassive stars, the total pressure is dominated by the radiation pressure contribution
\bm{[1,10]}
. The total pressure from a mixture of gas and radiation is
p=\mathscr{R}\rho\Theta+\frac{1}{3}a\Theta^{4}\sim\rho\Theta+\Theta^{4}
, where
\Theta
is the temperature of the gas. When
\Theta\sim\rho^{1/2}
then
p\sim\Theta^{4/3}
. Such stars are then teetering on the brink of catastrophic instability. The case
\gamma=4/3
for equilibrium configurations has been shown to be nonlinearly unstable even though there are no growing modes in the linearised analysis
\bm{28}
. Special dynamical solutions can be constructed for the
EP_{4/3}
system due to the self-similarity property. A compact fluid/gas sphere with
\gamma=4/3
can expand or contract in a self-similar manner by cascading between different scales with
t\rightarrow\lambda(t)
., preserving the total mass. When
\gamma=4/3
then
1/(2-\gamma)=\frac{3}{2}
. The self-similar expansion/collapse for
EP_{4/3}
would then have the rates

(6.77)

\displaystyle\overline{\lambda_{expand}(t)}\sim_{t\uparrow\infty}c_{1}t^{2/3},~~\overline{\lambda_{collapse}(t)}\sim_{t\uparrow\infty}c_{1}|T-t|^{2/3}

for some constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
, where
\overline{\lambda_{expand}(t)},\overline{\lambda_{collapse}(t)}
represent the radii of the expanding or collapsing star. Solutions from the works (REFS) expand and collapse at a linear rate nd are no self-similar with respect to rescaling so that

(6.78)

\displaystyle\overline{\lambda_{expand}(t)}\sim_{t\uparrow\infty}c_{1}t,~~\overline{\lambda_{collapse}(t)}\sim_{t\uparrow\infty}c_{1}|T-t|

6.1.
Hydrostatic equilibrium via the virial tensor

The fundamental equation of hydrostatic equilibrium can also be derived from the conservation of the virial tensor for a self-gravitating gas/fluid. The virial theorem for stars (Theorem 2.18) is also re-derived from this tensor. One first requires the following preliminary lemmas

Lemma 6.10
.

The time derivative of the product of the density and the velocity is

(6.79)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(u^{k}\rho)=-\big[\nabla_{i}(\rho u^{i}u^{k})+\delta^{ik}\nabla_{i}p+\rho\nabla^{k}\Phi\big]

Proof.

The EP equations are

(6.80)

\displaystyle u^{k}\partial_{t}\rho+u^{k}\nabla_{i}\rho u^{i}+u^{k}(\nabla_{i}u^{i})\rho=0

(6.81)

\displaystyle\rho\partial_{t}u^{k}+\rho u^{i}\nabla_{i}u^{k}+\delta^{ik}\nabla_{i}p=-\rho\nabla^{k}\Phi

where the continuity equation has been multiplied through by
u^{i}
. Adding these then gives (6.85)
∎

Definition 6.11
.

Let
\mathbf{D}(t)_{\subset}\mathbf{R}^{3}
support an
EP
system with density
\rho=\rho(\bm{x},t)
, and velocity
u^{i}
then the virial tensor is defined as

(6.82)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{V}}^{ij}(t)=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(x,t)x^{i}u^{j}dV(x)

and
\bm{\mathcal{V}}(t)=\bm{\mathcal{V}}^{ij}(t)\delta_{ij}
is the ’virial’.

Lemma 6.12
.

(
Derivative of the Newtonian potential
)

The fundamental solutions of the Laplace equation
\Delta\Gamma(x-y)=0
are (REF-Evans)

(6.83)

\displaystyle\Gamma(x-y)=\Gamma(|\bm{x}-\bm{y}|)=\frac{1}{n(2-n)V(\mathbf{B}(0,1))}|\bm{x}-\bm{y}|^{2-n},~~n>2

(6.84)

\displaystyle\Gamma(x-y)=\Gamma(|\bm{x}-\bm{y}|)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|\bm{x}-\bm{y}|,~~n=2

where
\Delta=\nabla_{i}\nabla^{i}=\partial^{2}/\partial x_{i}\partial x^{i}
. The following derivative estimates hold

(6.85)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\Gamma(\bm{x}-\bm{y})\leq\frac{1}{nV(\mathbf{B}(0,1))}|\bm{x}-\bm{y}|^{1-n}

(6.86)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\Gamma(\bm{x}-\bm{y})\leq\frac{1}{V(\mathbf{B}(0,1))}|\bm{x}-\bm{y}|^{-n}

Let
\rho:\mathbf{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
be a density function with support in
\bm{\mathbf{D}}\in\mathbf{R}^{3}
and let
\Phi(x)
be the Newtonian potential
of
\rho
. Then
\Phi\in C^{1}(\mathbf{R})
for all
x\in\bm{\mathbf{D}}\subset\mathbf{R}^{n}
. The derivatives of
\Phi(x)
are then

(6.87)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\Phi(x)=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\nabla_{i}\Gamma(\bm{x}-\bm{y})\rho(y)d^{n}y

(6.88)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\Phi(x)=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\Gamma(\bm{x}-\bm{y})\rho(y)d^{n}y

Now let
T\in[0,\infty),\rho\in C^{2}([0,T)\times\mathbf{R}^{3})
so that the Newtonian potential
\Phi(\bm{x},t)
at time t is

(6.89)

\displaystyle\Phi(\bm{x},t)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(y,t)}{|\bm{x}-\bm{y}|}d^{3}y,~x\in\mathbf{R}^{3}

The derivative is then
\bm{[24]}
,

(6.90)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\Phi(\bm{x},t)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbf{D}}\nabla_{i}\frac{\rho(y,t)}{|\bm{x}-\bm{y}|}d^{3}y=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(\bm{y},t)\frac{(x^{j}-y^{j})}{|x-y|^{3}}dV(y)

(6.91)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(\bm{y},t)\delta_{kj}\frac{(x^{k}-y^{k})}{|x-y|^{3}}dV(y)

Theorem 6.13
.

(
Fubin-Tonelli theorem
)

Let
f:\mathbf{R}^{3}\times\mathbf{R}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
be a multivariate function
f=f(x,y)
for
x\in X\subset\mathbf{R}^{+}
and

y\in Y\subset\mathbf{R}^{+}
. Then

(6.92)

\displaystyle\int_{X}\left(\int_{Y}f(x,y)d^{n}y\right)d^{n}x=\int_{Y}\left(\int_{X}f(x,y)d^{n}x\right)d^{n}y

with convergence

(6.93)

\displaystyle\int_{X}\left(\int_{Y}f(x,y)d^{n}y\right)d^{n}x<\infty

The gravitational energy
\mathbf{E}_{G}
of the EP system can also be expressed as an integral involving the gradient of the Newtonian potential. This lemma will be required when deriving the virial theorem for stars from the virial tensor
\mathbf{V}^{ij}
.

Lemma 6.14
.

Given
\mathbf{D}(t)\in\mathbf{R}^{3}
and the density
\rho(x,t)
and Newtonian potential
\Phi(x,t)
defined as before then

(6.94)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(x,t)\nabla_{j}\Phi(x,t)x^{j}dV(x)=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(x,t)\Phi(x,t)dV(x)\equiv-\mathbf{E}_{G}

Proof.

The proof
\bm{[24]}
utilises the Fubini-Tonelli Theorem and the expression for the gradient
\nabla^{k}\Phi(x,t)
and the fact that

x^{i}=(x^{i}-y^{i})+y^{i}
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(x,t)\nabla_{j}\Phi(x,t)x^{j}dV(x)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(\bm{x},t)\rho(\bm{y},t)x^{j}\delta_{kj}\frac{(x^{k}-y^{k})}{|x-y|^{3}}dV(x)dV(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(\bm{x},t)\rho(\bm{y},t)\delta_{kj}\frac{(x^{j}-y^{j})(x^{k}-y^{k})}{|x-y|^{3}}dV(x)dV(y)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(\bm{x},t)\rho(\bm{y},t)\delta_{kj}\frac{y^{j}(x^{k}-y^{k})}{|x-y|^{3}}dV(x)dV(y)

(6.95)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(\bm{x},t)\rho(\bm{y},t)\frac{(x^{j}-y^{j})(x^{j}-y^{j})}{|x-y|^{3}}dV(x)dV(y)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho({x},t)\rho(\bm{y},t)\delta_{kj}\frac{y^{j}(x^{k}-y^{k})}{|x-y|^{3}}dV(x)dV(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(x,t)\left(\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(y,t)\left(\frac{1}{|x-y|}\right)dV(y)\right)dV(x)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho({x},t)\rho(\bm{y},t)\delta_{kj}\frac{y^{j}(x^{k}-y^{k})}{|x-y|^{3}}dV(x)dV(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(x,t)\left(\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(y,t)\left(\frac{1}{|x-y|}\right)dV(y)\right)dV(x)

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(y,t)y^{j}\left(\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(x,t)\frac{\delta_{kj}(y^{k}-x^{k})}{|x-y|^{3}}dV(x)\right)dV(y)

(6.96)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(x,t)\Phi(x,t)dV(x)-\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(x,t)y^{j}\nabla_{j}\Phi(y,t)dV(y)

Moving the last term on the RHS to the LHS and changing variables then gives

(6.97)

\displaystyle 2\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(x,t)\nabla_{j}\Phi(x,t)x^{j}dV(x)=-\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho(x,t)\Phi(x,t)dV(x)\equiv-\mathbf{E}_{G}

from which (6.94) follows.
∎

The hydrostatic equilibrium equation for self-gravitating gaseous stars then follows from conservation of the virial tensor.

Proposition 6.15
.

If

(6.98)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d\bm{\mathcal{V}}^{ij}(t)}{dt}\right|_{u=0}=\frac{d}{dt}\left|\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(x,t)x^{i}u^{j}\delta_{ij}dV(x)\right|_{u=0}=0

or

(6.99)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d\bm{\mathcal{V}}^{ij}(t)}{dt}\right|+\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\partial_{t}(\rho u^{i}u^{k})\delta V(x)=0

then the fundamental hydrostatic equilibrium equation follows such that

(6.100)

\displaystyle\underbrace{\nabla_{i}p+\rho\nabla_{i}\Phi=0}

Proof.

The time derivative of the virial tensor is

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d\bm{\mathcal{V}}^{ij}(t)}{dt}\right|=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\partial_{t}\rho u^{i})x^{j}\delta_{ij}d^{3}x

(6.101)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\big[-\nabla_{i}(\rho u^{i}u^{k})-\delta^{ik}\nabla_{i}p-\rho\nabla^{k}\Phi\big]x^{j}\delta_{kj}dV(x)\delta_{ij}d^{3}x

using (6.77). Then if
u=0
and
\left|\frac{d\bm{\mathcal{V}}^{ij}(t)}{dt}\right|=0

(6.102)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d\bm{\mathcal{V}}^{ij}(t)}{dt}\right|=\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\big[-\delta^{ik}\nabla_{i}p-\rho\nabla^{k}\Phi\big]x^{j}\delta_{ik}d^{3}x=0

so that once again the following fundamental result is inevitable

(6.103)

\displaystyle\underbrace{\nabla^{k}p+\rho\nabla^{k}\Phi=0}

Theorem (2.8) gave the Virial Theorem for self-gravitating stars. The same result now follows from the conservation of the virial tensor via an integration by parts and utilisation of Lemma (6.14). Taking
u=0
as before for static equilibrium gives

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d\bm{\mathcal{V}}^{ij}(t)}{dt}\right|=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\partial_{t}\rho u^{i})x^{j}\delta_{ij}dV(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\big[-\delta^{ik}\nabla_{i}p-\rho\nabla^{k}\Phi\big]x^{j}\delta_{kj}dV(x)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}[\delta^{ik}\nabla_{i}px^{j}\delta_{kj}dV(x)-\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho\nabla^{k}\Phi x^{j}\delta_{kj}dV(x)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\underbrace{[\nabla^{k}px_{k}dV(x)}_{int~by~parts}-\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho\nabla^{k}\Phi x^{j}\delta_{kj}dV(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(\nabla_{k}x^{k})dV(x)-\underbrace{\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho\nabla^{k}\Phi x^{j}\delta_{kj}d^{3}}_{use~Lem~6.14}

(6.104)

\displaystyle=3\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(x)d^{3}x+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbf{D}(t)}\rho\phi(x)dV(x)=3\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(x)d^{3}x+\mathbf{E}_{G}=0

which is exactly equivalent to the stellar Virial Theorem (2.8).
∎

6.2.
Energy integrals for
EP_{\gamma}
systems in static equilibrium in all dimensions

Requires the following two preliminary lemmas.

Lemma 6.16
.

In a domain of n-dimensional Euclidean space,
{\mathbf{D}}\subset\mathbf{R}^{n}
the Poisson equation is

(6.105)

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{x})=\mathcal{Q}(n)\mathscr{G}\rho

The solution is

(6.106)

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{x})=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\Gamma(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\rho(\mathbf{y},t)d^{n}\mathbf{y}

where
\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})
is the Greens function or fundamental solution.

(1)

If
n=1
then

(6.107)

\displaystyle\Gamma(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})=|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|

(2)

If
n=2
then

(6.108)

\displaystyle\Gamma(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})=\log|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|

(3)

If
n\geq 3
then

(6.109)

\displaystyle\Gamma(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})=-\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{n-2}}

Since the pressure
p(x)=0
for all
\bm{x}\in\mathbf{D}
then the following useful integral identity can be derived.

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
\mathbf{D}\subset\mathbf{R}^{n}
and let
\partial\mathbf{D}
be the boundary. The domain supports a fluid/gas obeying the Euler-Poisson equations with pressure
p(\mathbf{x})
or
p(\mathbf{x},t)
, although here only the spatial dependence is relevant. At the boundary
p(\mathbf{x})=0
for all
\bm{x}\in\partial\mathbf{D}
. The gradient of the pressure is
\nabla p
. If
p(\mathbf{x})=0
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\partial\mathbf{D}
then

(6.110)

\displaystyle n\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(\mathbf{x})d^{n}\mathbf{x}=-\int_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{x}.\nabla p(\mathbf{x})d^{n}x

Proof.

Let
V(\mathbf{x})
be any vector field or vector in
\mathbf{D}\bigcup\partial\mathbf{D}
and let
p(x)
be the pressure. Then the basic identity for the divergence gives

(6.111)

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}.(fV)=f(\nabla.V)+V.\bm{\nabla}f\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial V_{i}}{\partial x^{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}V^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}p(x)

If
V(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
then

(6.112)

\displaystyle\nabla.(p(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x})=p(\mathbf{x})(\nabla.\mathbf{x})+\mathbf{x}.(\nabla f)=p(x)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial x_{i}}{\partial x_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\nabla^{i}p(\mathbf{x})=np(\mathbf{x})+\mathbf{x}.\bm{\nabla}p(\mathbf{x})

If
p(x)=0
on
\partial\mathbf{D}
then

(6.113)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{x}p(\mathbf{x})d^{n-1}x=0

Applying the divergence theorem and then (6.118) to this integral

(6.114)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{x}.p(\mathbf{x})d^{n-1}\mathbf{x}=\bm{\nabla}.(\mathbf{x}p(\mathbf{x})d^{n}\mathbf{x}=n\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(\mathbf{x})d^{n}\mathbf{x}+\int_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{x}.\bm{\nabla}f(\mathbf{x})d^{n}\mathbf{x}=0

so that

(6.115)

\displaystyle n\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(\mathbf{x})d^{n}\mathbf{x}=-\int_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{x}.\nabla p(\mathbf{x})d^{n}\mathbf{x}

as required.
∎

The following Lemma then applies the previous lemmas to establish the stationary energy integrals for an
EP_{\gamma}
system in dimensions
n=2
and
n\geq 3
. solutions to EP system for gaseous stars
\bm{[35]}

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
EP_{\gamma}
be an Euler-Poisson system for a polytropic gas with
p=\mathsf{K}|\rho|^{\gamma}
and with support
{\mathbf{D}}\subset\mathbf{R}^{3}
. On the boundary
p(x)=\rho(x)=0
for
x\in\partial\mathbf{D}
. As before, the total energy is

(6.116)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}(t)=\mathbf{E}_{K}(t)+\mathbf{E}(t)=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|u|^{2}dV(x)+\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi\right)d^{n}x,~\gamma>1

(6.117)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}(t)=\mathbf{E}_{K}(t)+\mathbf{E}(t)=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|u|^{2}dV(x)+\mathsf{K}\rho(x)\log\rho(x)+\frac{1}{2}\rho(x)\Phi(x)\right)dV(x),~\gamma=1

and the total mass is
M=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(x,t)d^{n}x
. The stationary energies are for
u=0
when
\mathbf{E}_{K}=0
so that
\mathbf{E}(t)=\mathbf{E}(t)
and the system is in hydrostatic equilibrium. Then when
\nabla_{i}p+\rho\nabla_{i}\Phi=0
the stationary energies are given by the following integral formulas.

(1)

For
n=2
or
\mathbf{D}\subset\mathbf{R}^{2}

(6.118)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}(t)=\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{4|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\int\!\!\!\int\rho(x)\rho(y)\log|x-y|d^{2}xd^{2}y

(2)

For
n\geq 3
or
\mathbf{D}\subset\mathbf{R}^{n}

(6.119)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}(t)=\frac{2(n-1)-n\gamma}{(n-2)(\gamma-1)}\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(x)dV(x)

Proof.

To prove (6.118), apply equation (6.115) with
n=2
so that

\displaystyle 2\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(x)d^{2}x=-\int_{\mathbf{D}}x.\nabla p(x)d^{2}x=\int_{\mathbf{D}}x\rho(x)\nabla\Phi(x)d^{2}x

\displaystyle=\mathscr{G}\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(x)\int_{\mathbf{D}}\nabla_{x}\log|x-y|\rho(y)d^{2}xd^{2}y

(6.120)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{G}\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(x)\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(y)(x-y).x}{|x-y|^{2}}d^{2}xd^{2}y\equiv\mathscr{G}\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)(x-y)^{2}.x}{|x-y|^{2}}d^{2}xd^{2}y=\mathscr{G}\mathbf{I\!H}

The integral
\mathbf{I\!H}
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathbf{I\!H}=\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)(x-y)^{2}.x}{|x-y|^{2}}d^{2}xd^{2}y

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)(x-y)(x-y)}{|x-y|^{2}}d^{2}xd^{2}y+\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)(x-y)^{2}.y}{|x-y|^{2}}d^{2}yd^{2}y

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(x)\rho(y)d^{2}xd^{2}y+\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)(x-y)^{2}.y}{|x-y|^{2}}d^{2}yd^{2}y

(6.121)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(x)d^{2}x\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(y)d^{2}xd^{2}y-\mathbf{I\!H}=M^{2}-\mathbf{I\!H}

so that
\mathbf{I\!H}=\frac{1}{2}M^{2}
. Then the pressure integral becomes

(6.122)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(x)d^{n}x=\frac{1}{4}\mathscr{G}M^{2}

The stationary energy is then

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\left(\frac{p}{|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\Phi\right)d^{n}x=\frac{1}{4}\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(x)\Phi(x)d^{2}x

(6.123)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\frac{\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{|\gamma-1|}+\frac{1}{2}\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(x)\rho(y)\log|x-y|d^{2}xd^{2}y

since

(6.124)

\displaystyle\Phi(x)=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(y)\log|x-y|d^{3}y

To prove (6.119) for
n\geq 3
we must first establish the identity

(6.125)

\displaystyle n\int_{\mathbf{D}}\mathsf{K}|\rho|^{\gamma}d^{n}x+n\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(x)d^{n}x=-\frac{1}{2}(n-2)\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(x)\Phi(x)d^{n}x

First evaluate
\rho(x)\nabla\Phi(x)
taking the derivative of the
gravitational potential for
n\geq 3
giving

(6.126)

\displaystyle\rho(x)\nabla\Phi(x)=-\mathscr{G}\rho(x)\int_{\mathbf{D}}\nabla_{x}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n-2}}\rho(y)d^{n}y=\mathscr{G}\rho(x)\int_{\Omega}\frac{(n-2)(x-y)\rho(y)}{|x-y|^{n}}

Multiply by
x
and integrate over
\bm{\Omega}
so that

(6.127)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{D}}x.\rho(x)\nabla\Phi(x)d^{n}x=\mathscr{G}(n-2)\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)(x-y).x}{|x-y|^{n}}

However, from the FEHE
\nabla p(x)=-\rho\nabla\Phi
so

(6.128)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{D}}x.\rho(x)\nabla\Phi(x)d^{n}x=-\int_{\mathbf{D}}x.\nabla p(x)d^{n}x=\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(x)d^{n}x

and so

(6.129)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(x)d^{n}x=\mathscr{G}(n-2)\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)(x-y).x}{|x-y|^{n}}d^{2}xd^{2}y=\mathscr{G}\mathbf{I\!H}

The integral
\mathbf{I\!H}
can be manipulated as

(6.130)

\displaystyle\mathbf{I\!H}

\displaystyle=(n-2)\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)(x-y).x}{|x-y|^{n}}d^{2}xd^{2}y

\displaystyle=(n-2)\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)(x-y).(x-y)}{|x-y|^{n}}d^{2}xd^{2}y+(n-2)\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)(x-y).y}{|x-y|^{n}}d^{2}xd^{2}y

\displaystyle=(n-2)\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)}{|x-y|^{n-2}}d^{2}xd^{2}y+(n-2)\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)(x-y).y}{|x-y|^{n}}d^{2}xd^{2}y

(6.131)

\displaystyle=(n-2)\int=\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)}{|x-y|^{n-2}}d^{2}xd^{2}y-\mathbf{I\!H}

so that

(6.132)

\displaystyle\mathbf{I\!H}=\frac{1}{2}(n-2)\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)d^{n}xd^{n}y}{|x-y|^{n-2}}

The pressure integral becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(x)d^{n}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(n-2)\mathscr{G}\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{\rho(x)\rho(y)}{|x-y|^{n-2}}d^{n}xd^{n}y

(6.133)

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{1}{2}(n-2)\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(x)\Phi(x)d^{n}x

The total stationary energy is then

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{p(x)}{|\gamma-1|}d^{n}x+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbf{D}}\rho(x)\Phi(x)d^{n}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\frac{p(x)}{|\gamma-1|}d^{n}x-\frac{n}{n-2}\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(x)d^{n}x

(6.134)

\displaystyle=\frac{2(n-1)-n\gamma}{|\gamma-1|(n-2)}\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(x)d^{n}x

∎

Corollary 6.19
.

For
n=3
the stationary energy is

(6.135)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}=\frac{3(\frac{4}{3}-\gamma)}{|\gamma-1|}\int_{\mathbf{D}}p(x)d^{3}x

so that
\mathbf{E}=0
for
\gamma=4/3
. This once again confirms that a polytropic gaseous star with
\gamma=4/3
is teetering on instability.

6.3.
Bounds on the pressure integral

The following theorem
\bm{[36]}
establishes bounds on the pressure integral
\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}p(x)d^{n}x
. If the star collapsed to zero size then the pressure integral would blow up. Conversely, if the pressure integral is constant, finite and bounded then the star is stable and in equilibrium. From (6.135)
z\mathbf{E}<0
if
\gamma>4/3
and
E\geq 0
if
\gamma\leq 4/3
so the star is stable for
\gamma>4/3
. Using the Hardy-Littlewood-Paley inequality once can derive a finite bound on the pressure integral for
\gamma>2(n-1)/n
.

Some preliminary definitions and lemmas are first required.

Definition 6.20
.

If
f\in L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
then the Fourier transform
(\mathscr{F},f)(\xi)
and its inverse is defined as

(6.136)

\displaystyle\widehat{f(\xi)}=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}f(x)e^{-ix\xi}d^{n}x

(6.137)

\displaystyle\widehat{f(x)}=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}f(\xi)e^{ix\xi}d^{n}\xi

with derivatives
(\nabla^{\alpha}f)(\xi)=(i\xi)^{\alpha}f(\xi)
and
\nabla^{2}\widehat{f(\xi)}=-\xi^{2}\widehat{f(\xi)}
.

Lemma 6.21
.

If
f\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
then Plancheral’s Thm states that
\|\mathscr{F}f(\bullet)\|_{L_{2}}=\|f(\bullet)\|_{L_{2}}
or
\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|f(x)|^{2}d^{n}x=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|f(\xi)|^{2}d^{\xi}
.

Lemma 6.22
.

The Fourier transforms of the density
\rho(x)
and the gravitational potential
\phi(x)
and their inverses are

(6.138)

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho(\xi)}=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(x)e^{-ix\xi}d^{n}x

(6.139)

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho(x)}=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(\xi)e^{ix\xi}d^{n}\xi

(6.140)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi(\xi)}=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\Phi(x)e^{-ix\xi}d^{n}x

(6.141)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi(x)}=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\Phi(\xi)e^{ix\xi}d^{n}\xi

so that

(6.142)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(x)\Phi(x)d^{n}x=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\widehat{\rho(\xi)}\widehat{\Phi(\xi)}d^{n}\xi

or

(6.143)

\displaystyle\|\rho(\bullet)\Phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{1}}=\|\widehat{\rho(\bullet)}\widehat{\Phi(\bullet)}\|_{L_{1}}

Proof.

Since
\Phi(x)
is real then
\Phi^{*}(x)=\Phi(x)
. The integral (6.142) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(x)\Phi(x)d^{n}x=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(x)\Phi^{*}(x)d^{n}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\left|\int\!\!int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(\xi)\Phi^{*}(\eta)e^{ix(\xi-\eta)}d^{n}\xi d^{n}\eta\right|d^{n}x

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\!\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(\xi)\Phi^{*}(\eta)\delta^{n}(\xi-\eta)d^{n}\xi d^{n}

(6.144)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(\xi)\Phi^{*}(\xi)d^{n}\xi=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(\xi)\Phi(\xi)d^{n}\xi

One an also use the Cauchy Schwartz inequality, taking the equality, and the Plancharel Theorem so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(x)\Phi(x)d^{n}x

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|\rho(x)|^{2}d^{n}x\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|\Phi(x)|^{2}d^{n}x\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|\rho(\xi)|^{2}d^{n}\xi\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|\Phi(\xi)|^{2}d^{n}\xi\right)^{1/2}

(6.145)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(\xi)\Phi(\xi)d^{n}\xi

∎

The final preliminary lemma is the Hardy Littlewood-Paley inequality.

Lemma 6.23
.

Let
1<\gamma\leq 2
and
f\in L^{\gamma}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
then
\exists
a
Marcinkiewicz interpolation constant
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}_{\gamma}
such that

(6.146)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|\widehat{f(\xi)}|^{\gamma}|\gamma|^{n(\gamma-2)}d^{n}\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\leq\bm{\mathfrak{M}}_{\gamma}\|f(\bullet)\|_{\gamma}\equiv\bm{\mathfrak{M}}_{\gamma}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|f(x)|^{\gamma}d^{n}x

If
f(\xi)=\rho(\xi)
then

(6.147)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|\widehat{\rho(\xi)}|^{\gamma}|\gamma|^{n(\gamma-2)}d^{n}\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\leq\bm{\mathfrak{M}}_{\gamma}\|\rho(\bullet)\|_{\gamma}\equiv\bm{\mathfrak{M}}_{\gamma}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|\rho(x)|^{\gamma}d^{n}x

The main theorem on the pressure integral bound is then as follows
\bm{[36]}
.

Theorem 6.24
.

Let
p=\mathsf{K}\rho^{\gamma}
be a polytropic gas equation of state and let
(p,\rho,u)
be a solution of an
EP_{\gamma}
system with support
\Omega
with mass
M
and total energy
\mathrm{E}
.

(1)

Then for
\gamma\geq 2(n-1)/n
there exists constants

C_{1}(M,E,\gamma)
and
C_{2}(M,E,\gamma)
such that

(6.148)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}p(x)d^{n}x\leq C_{1}(M,E,\gamma)=2(\gamma-1)\left[E-\frac{1}{2}n^{2}(n-1)V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1))\mathscr{G}M^{2}\gamma^{n-2}\right]<\infty

where
V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1))
is the volume of the unit ball.

(2)

For
2(n-1)/n<\gamma<2

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}p(x)d^{n}x\leq C_{2}(M,E,\gamma)\leq\frac{\mathsf{K}}{|\gamma-1|}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|\rho(x)|^{\gamma}d^{n}x+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}n(n-2)\mathscr{G}|\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\left|\frac{|\gamma-2}{\gamma-1}\right|M

(6.149)

\displaystyle+n^{2}(n-2)|\mathrm{V}(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|^{2}\mathscr{G}M^{2}\lambda^{n-2}=C_{2}(M,E,\gamma)<\infty

Proof.

We consider the case
2(n-1)/n<\gamma<2
then the case
\gamma>2
. The following identities will be useful in establishing the proof.

(6.150)

\displaystyle\xi^{2}=\xi^{2(n-\gamma)}\xi^{2+n(\gamma-2)}

(6.151)

\displaystyle\xi^{2}=|\xi|^{n-1}|\xi|^{3-n}

(6.152)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\xi|^{n-1}}\leq\frac{n}{\lambda^{n-1}},\lambda>0

(6.153)

\displaystyle\int_{\xi\leq|\lambda|}d^{n}\xi=|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\lambda^{n}

(6.154)

\displaystyle|\widehat{\rho(\xi)}|\leq\|\rho(\bullet)\|_{L_{1}}=M=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(x)d^{n}x

(1)

The case
2(n-1)/n<\gamma<2
. First take the Fourier transform of the Poisson equation in n dimensions so that

(6.155)

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}(\Delta\Phi(x))=n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1))|\mathscr{G}\rho(x)

which is

(6.156)

\displaystyle\Delta\widehat{\Phi(\xi)}=-\xi^{2}\Phi(\xi)=n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}\widehat{\rho(\xi)}

so that

(6.157)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi(\xi)}=-n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}\frac{\widehat{\rho(\xi)}}{|\xi|^{2}}

Multiplying by
\widehat{\rho(\xi)}
and integrating gives

(6.158)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(\xi)\Phi(\xi)d^{n}\xi=-n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\frac{\widehat{\rho(\xi)}}{|\xi|^{2}}d^{n}\xi

Then

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\rho(x)\Phi(x)d^{n}x=n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\frac{|\widehat{\rho(\xi)}|^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}d^{n}\xi

\displaystyle=n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}\int_{|\xi|>\lambda}\frac{|\widehat{\rho(\xi)}|^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}d^{n}\xi

\displaystyle+n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}\int_{|\xi|\leq\lambda}\frac{|\widehat{\rho(\xi)}|^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}d^{n}\xi

\displaystyle=n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}\int_{|\xi|>\lambda}\frac{|\widehat{\rho(\xi)}|^{2-\gamma}|\rho(\xi)|^{\gamma}}{|\xi|^{2}}d^{n}\xi

\displaystyle+n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}\int_{|\xi|\leq\lambda}\frac{|\widehat{\rho(\xi)}|^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}d^{n}\xi

\displaystyle\leq n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}\int_{|\xi|>\lambda}\frac{\|\widehat{\rho(\bullet)}\|_{L_{1}}^{2-\gamma}|\rho(\xi)|^{\gamma}}{|\xi|^{2}}d^{n}\xi

\displaystyle-n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}\int_{|\xi|\leq\lambda}\frac{\|\widehat{\rho(\bullet)}\|_{L_{1}}^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}d^{n}\xi

\displaystyle\leq-n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)M^{2-\gamma}|\mathscr{G}\int_{|\xi|>\lambda}\frac{\rho(\xi)|^{\gamma}}{|\xi|^{2}}d^{n}\xi

\displaystyle-n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}\int_{|\xi|\leq\lambda}\frac{\|\widehat{\rho(\bullet)}\|^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}d^{n}\xi

\displaystyle\leq-n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)M^{2-\gamma}|\mathscr{G}\int_{|\xi|>\lambda}\frac{\rho(\xi)|^{\gamma}}{|\xi|^{2}}d^{n}\xi

\displaystyle-n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}M^{2}\int_{|\xi|\leq\lambda}\frac{d^{n}\xi}{|\xi|^{2}}d^{n}\xi

\displaystyle=-n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)M^{2-\gamma}|\mathscr{G}\int_{|\xi|>\lambda}\frac{\rho(\xi)|^{\gamma}}{|\xi|^{n(2-\gamma})\xi^{2+n(\gamma-2)}}d^{n}\xi

\displaystyle-n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}M^{2}\int_{|\xi|\leq\lambda}\frac{d^{n}\xi}{|\xi|^{n-1}|\xi|^{3-n}}d^{n}\xi

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)M^{2-\gamma}|\mathscr{G}}{\lambda^{2+n(\gamma-2)}}\underbrace{\int_{|\xi|>\lambda}\frac{|\rho(\xi)|^{\gamma}}{|\xi|^{n(2-\gamma})}d^{n}}_{use~HLP~ineq.}\xi

\displaystyle-\frac{n(n-2)|V(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1)|\mathscr{G}M^{2}}{\lambda^{3-n}}\int_{|\xi|\leq\lambda}\frac{d^{n}\xi}{|\xi|^{n-1}}d^{n}\xi
